I'm not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I definitely hear the sound of their voice. My hearing is actually perfectly fine. We have to do these mandatory hearing tests at the plant every year because of OSHA rules, right? They stick you in this tiny little soundproof booth. It always reminds me of those old payphone booths. You don't really see those anymore, do you? I saw one at a gas station out on Route 9 a few months ago and it didn't even have a phone in it, just wires hanging out. Everybody just uses cell phones now anyway. 

But yeah, in the booth, you put on these huge headphones and you have to click a little button every time you hear a beep. I always pass with flying colors. Though last time the nurse said I had a little wax buildup. My buddy Dave—he drives the forklift on the loading dock—he told me I should try ear candling. Have you ever seen that? You light a hollow candle and stick it right in your ear canal. Sounds like a great way to set your hair on fire if you ask me. I told him he was crazy. 

But wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the words. Yeah, I don't know, I guess I just don't process it or something. Like, I hear the noise they're making, but the meaning just kind of slips right by me.

Losing focus when I'm alone? Uh, not really, I don't think. I mean, I don't read a whole lot of instructions anyway. I usually just figure things out as I go. And with TV... I mostly just have the game on or watch those car restoration shows.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Background noise? Um, I don't know, not really. I mean, the plant is loud, sure, but even when it's quiet I still miss things. Like, we could be sitting in the living room with the TV off and I still won't catch what my wife is saying. 

Speaking of the breakroom though, they just put in one of those fancy new vending machines. The ones with the glass front where you can see the little coils turn and drop the snacks down. It's supposed to have a sensor so if your chips get stuck, it gives you your money back. But last Tuesday, Dave—the forklift guy I was telling you about—he put in two dollars for a bag of those spicy pork rinds. The coil turned, the bag moved forward, and then it just hung there by the very top corner. So he shakes the machine, right? Which you're not supposed to do, there's a big yellow sticker on the front that says "WARNING: DO NOT TIP." Anyway, he shakes it, and the pork rinds don't fall, but a honey bun from the row above drops down. Dave hates honey buns. He was so mad he went and complained to HR about it. 

But yeah, anyway... what was the question? Oh, background noise. Yeah, I don't think it makes a difference. It's just hard to follow people no matter where we are.

Yeah, I was like this even as a kid. School was pretty rough. The teachers were always moving my seat around because I just couldn't stay put. I was always up and walking around or fidgeting. Dave—the forklift guy—he pointed it out and laughed at me for like ten minutes straight in the locker room. I felt like an idiot. I should probably get a new razor anyway, the blades on those disposable ones get dull so fast if you leave them in the shower. 

But, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, so way back before middle school, I was always getting in trouble. Mind drifting? Uh, I don't really think so. I mean, usually I'm trying to listen, but it's like I just miss the words somehow. It's not that I'm thinking about other stuff, you know? 

Though, speaking of thinking about other stuff, I was just trying to remember if I left the garage door open this morning. My neighbor, Gary, he's got this habit of just walking right into my garage if the door is up to borrow tools. Which, normally I don't care, but last time he took my good socket wrench set—the Craftsman one my dad gave me—and he left it sitting out on his back patio in the rain for three days. The case got all warped and a couple of the sockets started getting those little rust spots on them. I spent like two hours soaking them in WD-40 and scrubbing them with a wire brush trying to get it off. WD-40 is amazing stuff, really. You can use it for almost anything. I even used it to get some gum out of my niece's hair once at a family barbecue.

Found it sitting under a tarp in some guy's barn out in Ohio. The thing was completely rusted out, like, you could see straight through the floorboards to the dirt. He swore he was gonna fix it up himself, bought all this expensive welding gear and took over their whole two-car garage. Made my sister absolutely furious because she had to park her minivan out in the driveway all winter. And you know how bad the ice gets here in January. Scraping a windshield at 5:30 in the morning is just miserable. I finally bought her one of those brass-blade scrapers for Christmas—they work way better than the cheap plastic ones you get at the gas station. The plastic ones always snap right when you're pressing down on a thick patch of ice. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, losing focus on TV and stuff. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed any problems with that. If I'm watching something, I'm just watching it, you know? It's really just when people are talking to me that I seem to miss things.\n\nDoctor: Is it especially hard to understand what people are saying when there’s background noise, like in the plant breakroom or a busy store?\n\nPatient: Background noise? Um, I don't know, not really. I mean, the plant is loud, sure, but even when it's quiet I still miss things. Like, we could be sitting in the living room with the TV off and I still won't catch what my wife is saying. \n\nSpeaking of the breakroom though, they just put in one of those fancy new vending machines. The ones with the glass front where you can see the little coils turn and drop the snacks down. It's supposed to have a sensor so if your chips get stuck, it gives you your money back. But last Tuesday, Dave—the forklift guy I was telling you about—he put in two dollars for a bag of those spicy pork rinds. The coil turned, the bag moved forward, and then it just hung there by the very top corner. So he shakes the machine, right? Which you're not supposed to do, there's a big yellow sticker on the front that says \"WARNING: DO NOT TIP.\" Anyway, he shakes it, and the pork rinds don't fall, but a honey bun from the row above drops down. Dave hates honey buns. He was so mad he went and complained to HR about it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... what was the question? Oh, background noise. Yeah, I don't think it makes a difference. It's just hard to follow people no matter where we are.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I was like this even as a kid. School was pretty rough. The teachers were always moving my seat around because I just couldn't stay put. I was always up and walking around or fidgeting. They had those old desks, you know the ones where the chair is attached to the desk with that thick metal tube on the bottom? I used to always try to balance on the back legs of those things. My buddy Mike actually tipped all the way backward in fifth grade and slammed the back of his head right into the chalkboard tray. Man, there was so much blood. Head wounds always bleed like crazy, don't they? \n\nLike just last week I nicked the top of my ear while I was shaving my neck, and it just wouldn't stop bleeding. I had to stick a little piece of toilet paper on it, and I completely forgot about it and went to the plant with it still stuck to my ear. Dave—the forklift guy—he pointed it out and laughed at me for like ten minutes straight in the locker room. I felt like an idiot. I should probably get a new razor anyway, the blades on those disposable ones get dull so fast if you leave them in the shower. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, so way back before middle school, I was always getting in trouble. Besides not staying in my seat, I would always just call out the answers without raising my hand. And I'd constantly be leaning over and butting into whatever the other kids were working on instead of doing my own stuff. I just couldn't seem to help myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, do you find your mind drifting to other thoughts before they finish?\n\nPatient: Mind drifting? Uh, I don't really think so. I mean, usually I'm trying to listen, but it's like I just miss the words somehow. It's not that I'm thinking about other stuff, you know? \n\nThough, speaking of thinking about other stuff, I was just trying to remember if I left the garage door open this morning. My neighbor, Gary, he's got this habit of just walking right into my garage if the door is up to borrow tools. Which, normally I don't care, but last time he took my good socket wrench set—the Craftsman one my dad gave me—and he left it sitting out on his back patio in the rain for three days. The case got all warped and a couple of the sockets started getting those little rust spots on them. I spent like two hours soaking them in WD-40 and scrubbing them with a wire brush trying to get it off. WD-40 is amazing stuff, really. You can use it for almost anything. I even used it to get some gum out of my niece's hair once at a family barbecue. \n\nBut wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my mind drifting when people talk. Yeah, no, I don't really notice my mind wandering off or anything like that. I just... I don't know, I just don't catch what they're saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Forget things? No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty good about that stuff. My wife usually keeps the calendar on the fridge anyway, so I just look at that. 

Speaking of the fridge, though, we just had to get a new one last month. The old one started making this awful grinding noise, sounded like a bandsaw cutting through sheet metal every time the compressor kicked on. I tried to fix it myself first. I pulled it away from the wall—which, by the way, the amount of dust bunnies back there was insane, I even found a dog toy from our old lab, Buster, who passed away like three years ago. Man, he was a good dog. He used to love chasing the laser pointer. Anyway, I took the back panel off the fridge and figured out it was the condenser fan motor. I ordered the part online, but when it showed up, the wiring harness was totally different. Typical, right? They always change the design just enough so you can't fix the old models. So we ended up just going to Home Depot and buying a new one. It's got the ice maker in the door and everything, which is nice, except the crushed ice setting shoots ice chips all over the floor if you don't hold your cup up high enough.

But, uh... what did you ask me? Oh, forgetting appointments and stuff. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually remember where I put my keys and things like that.

Head wounds always bleed like crazy, don't they? \n\nLike just last week I nicked the top of my ear while I was shaving my neck, and it just wouldn't stop bleeding. I had to stick a little piece of toilet paper on it, and I completely forgot about it and went to the plant with it still stuck to my ear. Dave—the forklift guy—he pointed it out and laughed at me for like ten minutes straight in the locker room. I felt like an idiot. I should probably get a new razor anyway, the blades on those disposable ones get dull so fast if you leave them in the shower. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, so way back before middle school, I was always getting in trouble. Besides not staying in my seat, I would always just call out the answers without raising my hand. And I'd constantly be leaning over and butting into whatever the other kids were working on instead of doing my own stuff. I just couldn't seem to help myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, do you find your mind drifting to other thoughts before they finish?\n\nPatient: Mind drifting? Uh, I don't really think so. I mean, usually I'm trying to listen, but it's like I just miss the words somehow. It's not that I'm thinking about other stuff, you know? \n\nThough, speaking of thinking about other stuff, I was just trying to remember if I left the garage door open this morning. My neighbor, Gary, he's got this habit of just walking right into my garage if the door is up to borrow tools. Which, normally I don't care, but last time he took my good socket wrench set—the Craftsman one my dad gave me—and he left it sitting out on his back patio in the rain for three days. The case got all warped and a couple of the sockets started getting those little rust spots on them. I spent like two hours soaking them in WD-40 and scrubbing them with a wire brush trying to get it off. WD-40 is amazing stuff, really. You can use it for almost anything. I even used it to get some gum out of my niece's hair once at a family barbecue. \n\nBut wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my mind drifting when people talk. Yeah, no, I don't really notice my mind wandering off or anything like that. I just... I don't know, I just don't catch what they're saying.\n\nDoctor: Outside of conversations, do you often forget everyday things like appointments, where you put things, or errands you meant to do?\n\nPatient: Forget things? No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty good about that stuff. My wife usually keeps the calendar on the fridge anyway, so I just look at that. \n\nSpeaking of the fridge, though, we just had to get a new one last month. The old one started making this awful grinding noise, sounded like a bandsaw cutting through sheet metal every time the compressor kicked on. I tried to fix it myself first. I pulled it away from the wall—which, by the way, the amount of dust bunnies back there was insane, I even found a dog toy from our old lab, Buster, who passed away like three years ago. Man, he was a good dog. He used to love chasing the laser pointer. Anyway, I took the back panel off the fridge and figured out it was the condenser fan motor. I ordered the part online, but when it showed up, the wiring harness was totally different. Typical, right? They always change the design just enough so you can't fix the old models. So we ended up just going to Home Depot and buying a new one. It's got the ice maker in the door and everything, which is nice, except the crushed ice setting shoots ice chips all over the floor if you don't hold your cup up high enough.\n\nBut, uh... what did you ask me? Oh, forgetting appointments and stuff. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually remember where I put my keys and things like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen someone is talking to you, is it hard to stay focused on their words the whole time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that's actually exactly what brought me in. People keep telling me I'm not listening to them. Like, my supervisor at the plant or my wife will be talking right to my face, and a minute later I realize I only caught like half of what they said, and I have to ask them to repeat the whole thing. 

My wife gets so mad about it. Just the other day she was telling me about her sister's new boyfriend. Her sister, Sarah, she goes through boyfriends like water, I swear. The last guy she dated was this guy who owned a food truck that sold artisanal grilled cheese sandwiches. Which, I don't even know what makes a grilled cheese "artisanal," you know? Like, it's just cheese and bread. But he used all these fancy cheeses like gruyere and fontina. Fontina melts really well, actually. We bought a block of it for a New Year's party a couple years ago to make fondue. Fondue pots are such a hassle to clean though, all that burnt cheese stuck to the bottom. You have to soak it for days. 

But, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, staying focused when people talk. Yeah, it's really hard. I just don't catch it all, and then I have to ask them to say it again. It drives everybody crazy.

It's really just when people are talking to me that I seem to miss things.\n\nDoctor: Is it especially hard to understand what people are saying when there’s background noise, like in the plant breakroom or a busy store?\n\nPatient: Background noise? Um, I don't know, not really. I mean, the plant is loud, sure, but even when it's quiet I still miss things. Like, we could be sitting in the living room with the TV off and I still won't catch what my wife is saying. \n\nSpeaking of the breakroom though, they just put in one of those fancy new vending machines. The ones with the glass front where you can see the little coils turn and drop the snacks down. It's supposed to have a sensor so if your chips get stuck, it gives you your money back. But last Tuesday, Dave—the forklift guy I was telling you about—he put in two dollars for a bag of those spicy pork rinds. The coil turned, the bag moved forward, and then it just hung there by the very top corner. So he shakes the machine, right? Which you're not supposed to do, there's a big yellow sticker on the front that says \"WARNING: DO NOT TIP.\" Anyway, he shakes it, and the pork rinds don't fall, but a honey bun from the row above drops down. Dave hates honey buns. He was so mad he went and complained to HR about it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... what was the question? Oh, background noise. Yeah, I don't think it makes a difference. It's just hard to follow people no matter where we are.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I was like this even as a kid. School was pretty rough. The teachers were always moving my seat around because I just couldn't stay put. I was always up and walking around or fidgeting. They had those old desks, you know the ones where the chair is attached to the desk with that thick metal tube on the bottom? I used to always try to balance on the back legs of those things. My buddy Mike actually tipped all the way backward in fifth grade and slammed the back of his head right into the chalkboard tray. Man, there was so much blood. Head wounds always bleed like crazy, don't they? \n\nLike just last week I nicked the top of my ear while I was shaving my neck, and it just wouldn't stop bleeding. I had to stick a little piece of toilet paper on it, and I completely forgot about it and went to the plant with it still stuck to my ear. Dave—the forklift guy—he pointed it out and laughed at me for like ten minutes straight in the locker room. I felt like an idiot. I should probably get a new razor anyway, the blades on those disposable ones get dull so fast if you leave them in the shower. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, so way back before middle school, I was always getting in trouble. Besides not staying in my seat, I would always just call out the answers without raising my hand. And I'd constantly be leaning over and butting into whatever the other kids were working on instead of doing my own stuff. I just couldn't seem to help myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, do you find your mind drifting to other thoughts before they finish?\n\nPatient: Mind drifting? Uh, I don't really think so. I mean, usually I'm trying to listen, but it's like I just miss the words somehow. It's not that I'm thinking about other stuff, you know? \n\nThough, speaking of thinking about other stuff, I was just trying to remember if I left the garage door open this morning. My neighbor, Gary, he's got this habit of just walking right into my garage if the door is up to borrow tools. Which, normally I don't care, but last time he took my good socket wrench set—the Craftsman one my dad gave me—and he left it sitting out on his back patio in the rain for three days. The case got all warped and a couple of the sockets started getting those little rust spots on them. I spent like two hours soaking them in WD-40 and scrubbing them with a wire brush trying to get it off. WD-40 is amazing stuff, really. You can use it for almost anything. I even used it to get some gum out of my niece's hair once at a family barbecue. \n\nBut wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my mind drifting when people talk. Yeah, no, I don't really notice my mind wandering off or anything like that. I just... I don't know, I just don't catch what they're saying.\n\nDoctor: Outside of conversations, do you often forget everyday things like appointments, where you put things, or errands you meant to do?\n\nPatient: Forget things? No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty good about that stuff. My wife usually keeps the calendar on the fridge anyway, so I just look at that. \n\nSpeaking of the fridge, though, we just had to get a new one last month. The old one started making this awful grinding noise, sounded like a bandsaw cutting through sheet metal every time the compressor kicked on. I tried to fix it myself first. I pulled it away from the wall—which, by the way, the amount of dust bunnies back there was insane, I even found a dog toy from our old lab, Buster, who passed away like three years ago. Man, he was a good dog. He used to love chasing the laser pointer. Anyway, I took the back panel off the fridge and figured out it was the condenser fan motor. I ordered the part online, but when it showed up, the wiring harness was totally different. Typical, right? They always change the design just enough so you can't fix the old models. So we ended up just going to Home Depot and buying a new one. It's got the ice maker in the door and everything, which is nice, except the crushed ice setting shoots ice chips all over the floor if you don't hold your cup up high enough.\n\nBut, uh... what did you ask me? Oh, forgetting appointments and stuff. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually remember where I put my keys and things like that.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, is it hard to stay focused on their words the whole time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's actually exactly what brought me in. People keep telling me I'm not listening to them. Like, it's just cheese and bread. But he used all these fancy cheeses like gruyere and fontina. Fontina melts really well, actually. We bought a block of it for a New Year's party a couple years ago to make fondue. Fondue pots are such a hassle to clean though, all that burnt cheese stuck to the bottom. You have to soak it for days. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, staying focused when people talk. Yeah, it's really hard. I just don't catch it all, and then I have to ask them to say it again. It drives everybody crazy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, like I was just saying, that's the whole reason I'm here. It happens all the time. My supervisor at the plant, or my wife, they'll be standing right in front of me, talking straight to my face, and I just... I've been thinking about getting those blue-light blocking glasses, but I don't know if they actually work or if it's just a scam.

He swore he was gonna fix it up himself, bought all this expensive welding gear and took over their whole two-car garage. Scraping a windshield at 5:30 in the morning is just miserable. I finally bought her one of those brass-blade scrapers for Christmas—they work way better than the cheap plastic ones you get at the gas station. The plastic ones always snap right when you're pressing down on a thick patch of ice. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, losing focus on TV and stuff. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed any problems with that. If I'm watching something, I'm just watching it, you know? It's really just when people are talking to me that I seem to miss things.\n\nDoctor: Is it especially hard to understand what people are saying when there’s background noise, like in the plant breakroom or a busy store?\n\nPatient: Background noise? Um, I don't know, not really. I mean, the plant is loud, sure, but even when it's quiet I still miss things. Like, we could be sitting in the living room with the TV off and I still won't catch what my wife is saying. \n\nSpeaking of the breakroom though, they just put in one of those fancy new vending machines. The ones with the glass front where you can see the little coils turn and drop the snacks down. It's supposed to have a sensor so if your chips get stuck, it gives you your money back. But last Tuesday, Dave—the forklift guy I was telling you about—he put in two dollars for a bag of those spicy pork rinds. The coil turned, the bag moved forward, and then it just hung there by the very top corner. So he shakes the machine, right? Which you're not supposed to do, there's a big yellow sticker on the front that says \"WARNING: DO NOT TIP.\" Anyway, he shakes it, and the pork rinds don't fall, but a honey bun from the row above drops down. Dave hates honey buns. He was so mad he went and complained to HR about it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... what was the question? Oh, background noise. Yeah, I don't think it makes a difference. It's just hard to follow people no matter where we are.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I was like this even as a kid. School was pretty rough. The teachers were always moving my seat around because I just couldn't stay put. I was always up and walking around or fidgeting. They had those old desks, you know the ones where the chair is attached to the desk with that thick metal tube on the bottom? I used to always try to balance on the back legs of those things. My buddy Mike actually tipped all the way backward in fifth grade and slammed the back of his head right into the chalkboard tray. Man, there was so much blood. Head wounds always bleed like crazy, don't they? \n\nLike just last week I nicked the top of my ear while I was shaving my neck, and it just wouldn't stop bleeding. I had to stick a little piece of toilet paper on it, and I completely forgot about it and went to the plant with it still stuck to my ear. Dave—the forklift guy—he pointed it out and laughed at me for like ten minutes straight in the locker room. I felt like an idiot. I should probably get a new razor anyway, the blades on those disposable ones get dull so fast if you leave them in the shower. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, so way back before middle school, I was always getting in trouble. Besides not staying in my seat, I would always just call out the answers without raising my hand. And I'd constantly be leaning over and butting into whatever the other kids were working on instead of doing my own stuff. I just couldn't seem to help myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, do you find your mind drifting to other thoughts before they finish?\n\nPatient: Mind drifting? Uh, I don't really think so. I mean, usually I'm trying to listen, but it's like I just miss the words somehow. It's not that I'm thinking about other stuff, you know? \n\nThough, speaking of thinking about other stuff, I was just trying to remember if I left the garage door open this morning. My neighbor, Gary, he's got this habit of just walking right into my garage if the door is up to borrow tools. Which, normally I don't care, but last time he took my good socket wrench set—the Craftsman one my dad gave me—and he left it sitting out on his back patio in the rain for three days. The case got all warped and a couple of the sockets started getting those little rust spots on them. I spent like two hours soaking them in WD-40 and scrubbing them with a wire brush trying to get it off. WD-40 is amazing stuff, really. You can use it for almost anything. I even used it to get some gum out of my niece's hair once at a family barbecue. \n\nBut wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my mind drifting when people talk. Yeah, no, I don't really notice my mind wandering off or anything like that. I just... I don't know, I just don't catch what they're saying.\n\nDoctor: Outside of conversations, do you often forget everyday things like appointments, where you put things, or errands you meant to do?\n\nPatient: Forget things? No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty good about that stuff. My wife usually keeps the calendar on the fridge anyway, so I just look at that. \n\nSpeaking of the fridge, though, we just had to get a new one last month. The old one started making this awful grinding noise, sounded like a bandsaw cutting through sheet metal every time the compressor kicked on. I tried to fix it myself first. I pulled it away from the wall—which, by the way, the amount of dust bunnies back there was insane, I even found a dog toy from our old lab, Buster, who passed away like three years ago. Man, he was a good dog. He used to love chasing the laser pointer. Anyway, I took the back panel off the fridge and figured out it was the condenser fan motor. I ordered the part online, but when it showed up, the wiring harness was totally different. Typical, right? They always change the design just enough so you can't fix the old models. So we ended up just going to Home Depot and buying a new one. It's got the ice maker in the door and everything, which is nice, except the crushed ice setting shoots ice chips all over the floor if you don't hold your cup up high enough.\n\nBut, uh... what did you ask me? Oh, forgetting appointments and stuff. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually remember where I put my keys and things like that.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, is it hard to stay focused on their words the whole time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's actually exactly what brought me in. People keep telling me I'm not listening to them. Like, my supervisor at the plant or my wife will be talking right to my face, and a minute later I realize I only caught like half of what they said, and I have to ask them to repeat the whole thing. \n\nMy wife gets so mad about it. Just the other day she was telling me about her sister's new boyfriend. Her sister, Sarah, she goes through boyfriends like water, I swear. The last guy she dated was this guy who owned a food truck that sold artisanal grilled cheese sandwiches. Which, I don't even know what makes a grilled cheese \"artisanal,\" you know? Like, it's just cheese and bread. But he used all these fancy cheeses like gruyere and fontina. Fontina melts really well, actually. We bought a block of it for a New Year's party a couple years ago to make fondue. Fondue pots are such a hassle to clean though, all that burnt cheese stuck to the bottom. You have to soak it for days. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, staying focused when people talk. Yeah, it's really hard. I just don't catch it all, and then I have to ask them to say it again. It drives everybody crazy.\n\nDoctor: When someone is speaking to you face-to-face, do you often realize you were distracted and not fully listening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, that's the whole reason I'm here. It happens all the time. My supervisor at the plant, or my wife, they'll be standing right in front of me, talking straight to my face, and I just... I don't know, a minute goes by and I realize I completely missed half of it. Then I have to stand there and ask them to repeat the whole thing. \n\nIt's actually really embarrassing at work. We had this big safety meeting last week because some guy on second shift got his sleeve caught in the conveyor belt. Which is crazy, because they have those big red emergency stop buttons like every ten feet along the line. You'd think someone would have just smacked one of those right away. I remember when they installed those buttons, it was right after OSHA came through and did this massive audit. The OSHA inspector was this really intense guy with a clipboard who kept checking the lighting levels with this little meter. He said the lumens were too low over by the loading dock. So maintenance had to come in over the weekend and swap out all the old fluorescent tubes for LEDs. The LEDs are so much brighter, honestly, it kind of gives me a headache by the end of the shift. I've been thinking about getting those blue-light blocking glasses, but I don't know if they actually work or if it's just a scam.\n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask? Oh, yeah, realizing I wasn't listening. Yeah, it happens constantly. I just realize I tuned out and I have to ask them to say it again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I finally bought her one of those brass-blade scrapers for Christmas—they work way better than the cheap plastic ones you get at the gas station. The plastic ones always snap right when you're pressing down on a thick patch of ice. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, losing focus on TV and stuff. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed any problems with that. If I'm watching something, I'm just watching it, you know? It's really just when people are talking to me that I seem to miss things.\n\nDoctor: Is it especially hard to understand what people are saying when there’s background noise, like in the plant breakroom or a busy store?\n\nPatient: Background noise? Um, I don't know, not really. I mean, the plant is loud, sure, but even when it's quiet I still miss things. Like, we could be sitting in the living room with the TV off and I still won't catch what my wife is saying. \n\nSpeaking of the breakroom though, they just put in one of those fancy new vending machines. The ones with the glass front where you can see the little coils turn and drop the snacks down. It's supposed to have a sensor so if your chips get stuck, it gives you your money back. But last Tuesday, Dave—the forklift guy I was telling you about—he put in two dollars for a bag of those spicy pork rinds. The coil turned, the bag moved forward, and then it just hung there by the very top corner. So he shakes the machine, right? Which you're not supposed to do, there's a big yellow sticker on the front that says \"WARNING: DO NOT TIP.\" Anyway, he shakes it, and the pork rinds don't fall, but a honey bun from the row above drops down. Dave hates honey buns. He was so mad he went and complained to HR about it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... what was the question? Oh, background noise. Yeah, I don't think it makes a difference. It's just hard to follow people no matter where we are.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I was like this even as a kid. School was pretty rough. The teachers were always moving my seat around because I just couldn't stay put. I was always up and walking around or fidgeting. They had those old desks, you know the ones where the chair is attached to the desk with that thick metal tube on the bottom? I used to always try to balance on the back legs of those things. My buddy Mike actually tipped all the way backward in fifth grade and slammed the back of his head right into the chalkboard tray. Man, there was so much blood. Head wounds always bleed like crazy, don't they? \n\nLike just last week I nicked the top of my ear while I was shaving my neck, and it just wouldn't stop bleeding. I had to stick a little piece of toilet paper on it, and I completely forgot about it and went to the plant with it still stuck to my ear. Dave—the forklift guy—he pointed it out and laughed at me for like ten minutes straight in the locker room. I felt like an idiot. I should probably get a new razor anyway, the blades on those disposable ones get dull so fast if you leave them in the shower. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, so way back before middle school, I was always getting in trouble. Besides not staying in my seat, I would always just call out the answers without raising my hand. And I'd constantly be leaning over and butting into whatever the other kids were working on instead of doing my own stuff. I just couldn't seem to help myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, do you find your mind drifting to other thoughts before they finish?\n\nPatient: Mind drifting? Uh, I don't really think so. I mean, usually I'm trying to listen, but it's like I just miss the words somehow. It's not that I'm thinking about other stuff, you know? \n\nThough, speaking of thinking about other stuff, I was just trying to remember if I left the garage door open this morning. My neighbor, Gary, he's got this habit of just walking right into my garage if the door is up to borrow tools. Which, normally I don't care, but last time he took my good socket wrench set—the Craftsman one my dad gave me—and he left it sitting out on his back patio in the rain for three days. The case got all warped and a couple of the sockets started getting those little rust spots on them. I spent like two hours soaking them in WD-40 and scrubbing them with a wire brush trying to get it off. WD-40 is amazing stuff, really. You can use it for almost anything. I even used it to get some gum out of my niece's hair once at a family barbecue. \n\nBut wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my mind drifting when people talk. Yeah, no, I don't really notice my mind wandering off or anything like that. I just... I don't know, I just don't catch what they're saying.\n\nDoctor: Outside of conversations, do you often forget everyday things like appointments, where you put things, or errands you meant to do?\n\nPatient: Forget things? No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty good about that stuff. My wife usually keeps the calendar on the fridge anyway, so I just look at that. \n\nSpeaking of the fridge, though, we just had to get a new one last month. The old one started making this awful grinding noise, sounded like a bandsaw cutting through sheet metal every time the compressor kicked on. I tried to fix it myself first. I pulled it away from the wall—which, by the way, the amount of dust bunnies back there was insane, I even found a dog toy from our old lab, Buster, who passed away like three years ago. Man, he was a good dog. He used to love chasing the laser pointer. Anyway, I took the back panel off the fridge and figured out it was the condenser fan motor. I ordered the part online, but when it showed up, the wiring harness was totally different. Typical, right? They always change the design just enough so you can't fix the old models. So we ended up just going to Home Depot and buying a new one. It's got the ice maker in the door and everything, which is nice, except the crushed ice setting shoots ice chips all over the floor if you don't hold your cup up high enough.\n\nBut, uh... what did you ask me? Oh, forgetting appointments and stuff. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually remember where I put my keys and things like that.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, is it hard to stay focused on their words the whole time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's actually exactly what brought me in. People keep telling me I'm not listening to them. Like, my supervisor at the plant or my wife will be talking right to my face, and a minute later I realize I only caught like half of what they said, and I have to ask them to repeat the whole thing. \n\nMy wife gets so mad about it. Just the other day she was telling me about her sister's new boyfriend. Her sister, Sarah, she goes through boyfriends like water, I swear. The last guy she dated was this guy who owned a food truck that sold artisanal grilled cheese sandwiches. Which, I don't even know what makes a grilled cheese \"artisanal,\" you know? Like, it's just cheese and bread. But he used all these fancy cheeses like gruyere and fontina. Fontina melts really well, actually. We bought a block of it for a New Year's party a couple years ago to make fondue. Fondue pots are such a hassle to clean though, all that burnt cheese stuck to the bottom. You have to soak it for days. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, staying focused when people talk. Yeah, it's really hard. I just don't catch it all, and then I have to ask them to say it again. It drives everybody crazy.\n\nDoctor: When someone is speaking to you face-to-face, do you often realize you were distracted and not fully listening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, that's the whole reason I'm here. It happens all the time. My supervisor at the plant, or my wife, they'll be standing right in front of me, talking straight to my face, and I just... I don't know, a minute goes by and I realize I completely missed half of it. Then I have to stand there and ask them to repeat the whole thing. \n\nIt's actually really embarrassing at work. We had this big safety meeting last week because some guy on second shift got his sleeve caught in the conveyor belt. Which is crazy, because they have those big red emergency stop buttons like every ten feet along the line. You'd think someone would have just smacked one of those right away. I remember when they installed those buttons, it was right after OSHA came through and did this massive audit. The OSHA inspector was this really intense guy with a clipboard who kept checking the lighting levels with this little meter. He said the lumens were too low over by the loading dock. So maintenance had to come in over the weekend and swap out all the old fluorescent tubes for LEDs. The LEDs are so much brighter, honestly, it kind of gives me a headache by the end of the shift. I've been thinking about getting those blue-light blocking glasses, but I don't know if they actually work or if it's just a scam.\n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask? Oh, yeah, realizing I wasn't listening. Yeah, it happens constantly. I just realize I tuned out and I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: When your wife is talking to you one-on-one in a quiet room, do you often catch yourself getting distracted before she finishes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, doc, I feel like a broken record here, but yes, exactly. I guess it has the primer mixed in, which saves a step, but I always feel like you should do a separate primer coat anyway, especially if you're painting over a dark color. The old color was this dark maroon, which made the room look like an absolute cave. We had to do three coats of that green to cover it up. My shoulder was killing me by the end of the weekend. I probably should have used a roller with a longer extension pole, but... \n\nWait, sorry. What were we just talking about? Oh, right, getting distracted with my wife. Yeah, same exact thing happens. Organized? No, not really. I actually don't think I have much of a problem with that. Like at work, my station is always set up right, and at home I keep my stuff pretty straight. I have this big red tool chest out in the garage where I keep all my wrenches sorted by size.

Yeah, he'll be talking right to me, and a minute later I realize I only caught like half of it. It’s the exact same with my wife. We were at Home Depot this weekend looking at patio chairs because ours got completely wrecked in that bad storm a couple weeks ago—the wind just picked the umbrella up and smashed the glass table everywhere, it was a huge mess to clean up. But yeah, she’ll be talking right to my face and I end up having to ask her to say it all over again because I just missed it. It's causing a lot of friction.\n\nDoctor: When people are talking to you, does it usually feel like you can hear the sound of their voice but the words don’t register, or does it feel like you can’t hear them clearly in the first place?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I definitely hear the sound of their voice. My hearing is actually perfectly fine. We have to do these mandatory hearing tests at the plant every year because of OSHA rules, right? They stick you in this tiny little soundproof booth. It always reminds me of those old payphone booths. You don't really see those anymore, do you? I saw one at a gas station out on Route 9 a few months ago and it didn't even have a phone in it, just wires hanging out. Everybody just uses cell phones now anyway. \n\nBut yeah, in the booth, you put on these huge headphones and you have to click a little button every time you hear a beep. I always pass with flying colors. Though last time the nurse said I had a little wax buildup. My buddy Dave—he drives the forklift on the loading dock—he told me I should try ear candling. Have you ever seen that? You light a hollow candle and stick it right in your ear canal. Sounds like a great way to set your hair on fire if you ask me. I told him he was crazy. \n\nBut wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the words. Yeah, I don't know, I guess I just don't process it or something. Like, I hear the noise they're making, but the meaning just kind of slips right by me.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself losing focus when you're doing things alone, like reading instructions or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Losing focus when I'm alone? Uh, not really, I don't think. I mean, I don't read a whole lot of instructions anyway. I usually just figure things out as I go. And with TV... I mostly just have the game on or watch those car restoration shows. \n\nHave you seen the ones where they rebuild old muscle cars? My brother-in-law actually bought a '68 Chevelle a few years back. Found it sitting under a tarp in some guy's barn out in Ohio. The thing was completely rusted out, like, you could see straight through the floorboards to the dirt. He swore he was gonna fix it up himself, bought all this expensive welding gear and took over their whole two-car garage. Made my sister absolutely furious because she had to park her minivan out in the driveway all winter. And you know how bad the ice gets here in January. Scraping a windshield at 5:30 in the morning is just miserable. I finally bought her one of those brass-blade scrapers for Christmas—they work way better than the cheap plastic ones you get at the gas station. The plastic ones always snap right when you're pressing down on a thick patch of ice. \n\nBut, uh... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, losing focus on TV and stuff. Yeah, no, I haven't really noticed any problems with that. If I'm watching something, I'm just watching it, you know? It's really just when people are talking to me that I seem to miss things.\n\nDoctor: Is it especially hard to understand what people are saying when there’s background noise, like in the plant breakroom or a busy store?\n\nPatient: Background noise? Um, I don't know, not really. I mean, the plant is loud, sure, but even when it's quiet I still miss things. Like, we could be sitting in the living room with the TV off and I still won't catch what my wife is saying. \n\nSpeaking of the breakroom though, they just put in one of those fancy new vending machines. The ones with the glass front where you can see the little coils turn and drop the snacks down. It's supposed to have a sensor so if your chips get stuck, it gives you your money back. But last Tuesday, Dave—the forklift guy I was telling you about—he put in two dollars for a bag of those spicy pork rinds. The coil turned, the bag moved forward, and then it just hung there by the very top corner. So he shakes the machine, right? Which you're not supposed to do, there's a big yellow sticker on the front that says \"WARNING: DO NOT TIP.\" Anyway, he shakes it, and the pork rinds don't fall, but a honey bun from the row above drops down. Dave hates honey buns. He was so mad he went and complained to HR about it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... what was the question? Oh, background noise. Yeah, I don't think it makes a difference. It's just hard to follow people no matter where we are.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention in school when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I was like this even as a kid. School was pretty rough. The teachers were always moving my seat around because I just couldn't stay put. I was always up and walking around or fidgeting. They had those old desks, you know the ones where the chair is attached to the desk with that thick metal tube on the bottom? I used to always try to balance on the back legs of those things. My buddy Mike actually tipped all the way backward in fifth grade and slammed the back of his head right into the chalkboard tray. Man, there was so much blood. Head wounds always bleed like crazy, don't they? \n\nLike just last week I nicked the top of my ear while I was shaving my neck, and it just wouldn't stop bleeding. I had to stick a little piece of toilet paper on it, and I completely forgot about it and went to the plant with it still stuck to my ear. Dave—the forklift guy—he pointed it out and laughed at me for like ten minutes straight in the locker room. I felt like an idiot. I should probably get a new razor anyway, the blades on those disposable ones get dull so fast if you leave them in the shower. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, being a kid. Yeah, so way back before middle school, I was always getting in trouble. Besides not staying in my seat, I would always just call out the answers without raising my hand. And I'd constantly be leaning over and butting into whatever the other kids were working on instead of doing my own stuff. I just couldn't seem to help myself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, do you find your mind drifting to other thoughts before they finish?\n\nPatient: Mind drifting? Uh, I don't really think so. I mean, usually I'm trying to listen, but it's like I just miss the words somehow. It's not that I'm thinking about other stuff, you know? \n\nThough, speaking of thinking about other stuff, I was just trying to remember if I left the garage door open this morning. My neighbor, Gary, he's got this habit of just walking right into my garage if the door is up to borrow tools. Which, normally I don't care, but last time he took my good socket wrench set—the Craftsman one my dad gave me—and he left it sitting out on his back patio in the rain for three days. The case got all warped and a couple of the sockets started getting those little rust spots on them. I spent like two hours soaking them in WD-40 and scrubbing them with a wire brush trying to get it off. WD-40 is amazing stuff, really. You can use it for almost anything. I even used it to get some gum out of my niece's hair once at a family barbecue. \n\nBut wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my mind drifting when people talk. Yeah, no, I don't really notice my mind wandering off or anything like that. I just... I don't know, I just don't catch what they're saying.\n\nDoctor: Outside of conversations, do you often forget everyday things like appointments, where you put things, or errands you meant to do?\n\nPatient: Forget things? No, not really. I mean, I'm pretty good about that stuff. My wife usually keeps the calendar on the fridge anyway, so I just look at that. \n\nSpeaking of the fridge, though, we just had to get a new one last month. The old one started making this awful grinding noise, sounded like a bandsaw cutting through sheet metal every time the compressor kicked on. I tried to fix it myself first. I pulled it away from the wall—which, by the way, the amount of dust bunnies back there was insane, I even found a dog toy from our old lab, Buster, who passed away like three years ago. Man, he was a good dog. He used to love chasing the laser pointer. Anyway, I took the back panel off the fridge and figured out it was the condenser fan motor. I ordered the part online, but when it showed up, the wiring harness was totally different. Typical, right? They always change the design just enough so you can't fix the old models. So we ended up just going to Home Depot and buying a new one. It's got the ice maker in the door and everything, which is nice, except the crushed ice setting shoots ice chips all over the floor if you don't hold your cup up high enough.\n\nBut, uh... what did you ask me? Oh, forgetting appointments and stuff. Yeah, no, I don't really have a problem with that. I usually remember where I put my keys and things like that.\n\nDoctor: When someone is talking to you, is it hard to stay focused on their words the whole time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's actually exactly what brought me in. People keep telling me I'm not listening to them. Like, my supervisor at the plant or my wife will be talking right to my face, and a minute later I realize I only caught like half of what they said, and I have to ask them to repeat the whole thing. \n\nMy wife gets so mad about it. Just the other day she was telling me about her sister's new boyfriend. Her sister, Sarah, she goes through boyfriends like water, I swear. The last guy she dated was this guy who owned a food truck that sold artisanal grilled cheese sandwiches. Which, I don't even know what makes a grilled cheese \"artisanal,\" you know? Like, it's just cheese and bread. But he used all these fancy cheeses like gruyere and fontina. Fontina melts really well, actually. We bought a block of it for a New Year's party a couple years ago to make fondue. Fondue pots are such a hassle to clean though, all that burnt cheese stuck to the bottom. You have to soak it for days. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, right, staying focused when people talk. Yeah, it's really hard. I just don't catch it all, and then I have to ask them to say it again. It drives everybody crazy.\n\nDoctor: When someone is speaking to you face-to-face, do you often realize you were distracted and not fully listening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, that's the whole reason I'm here. It happens all the time. My supervisor at the plant, or my wife, they'll be standing right in front of me, talking straight to my face, and I just... I don't know, a minute goes by and I realize I completely missed half of it. Then I have to stand there and ask them to repeat the whole thing. \n\nIt's actually really embarrassing at work. We had this big safety meeting last week because some guy on second shift got his sleeve caught in the conveyor belt. Which is crazy, because they have those big red emergency stop buttons like every ten feet along the line. You'd think someone would have just smacked one of those right away. I remember when they installed those buttons, it was right after OSHA came through and did this massive audit. The OSHA inspector was this really intense guy with a clipboard who kept checking the lighting levels with this little meter. He said the lumens were too low over by the loading dock. So maintenance had to come in over the weekend and swap out all the old fluorescent tubes for LEDs. The LEDs are so much brighter, honestly, it kind of gives me a headache by the end of the shift. I've been thinking about getting those blue-light blocking glasses, but I don't know if they actually work or if it's just a scam.\n\nWait, sorry, what did you ask? Oh, yeah, realizing I wasn't listening. Yeah, it happens constantly. I just realize I tuned out and I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: When your wife is talking to you one-on-one in a quiet room, do you often catch yourself getting distracted before she finishes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, doc, I feel like a broken record here, but yes, exactly. Like I was saying before, that's the whole reason I came in. Even if it's just me and her in the living room and it's dead quiet, she'll be talking straight to me and I still end up only catching half of it. Then I'm asking her to say the whole thing over again. \n\nIt really doesn't even matter if it's quiet. We actually just painted that living room to make it more relaxing, you know? This light sage green color. The guy at the hardware store said it was their most popular color this year. Paint is so expensive now, though, I couldn't believe it. Almost eighty bucks a gallon for the premium stuff. I guess it has the primer mixed in, which saves a step, but I always feel like you should do a separate primer coat anyway, especially if you're painting over a dark color. The old color was this dark maroon, which made the room look like an absolute cave. We had to do three coats of that green to cover it up. My shoulder was killing me by the end of the weekend. I probably should have used a roller with a longer extension pole, but... \n\nWait, sorry. What were we just talking about? Oh, right, getting distracted with my wife. Yeah, same exact thing happens. I just tune out and have to ask her what she said.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you also have trouble staying organized at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Organized? No, not really. I actually don't think I have much of a problem with that. Like at work, my station is always set up right, and at home I keep my stuff pretty straight. I have this big red tool chest out in the garage where I keep all my wrenches sorted by size. \n\nI actually bought that tool chest at a yard sale a few years back. It was a total steal, honestly. The guy selling it was moving down to Florida to retire. I don't know why everybody wants to move to Florida as soon as they hit sixty-five. The humidity down there is just brutal. My brother took his kids to Orlando for vacation last summer and he said it was like walking through hot soup the whole time. Plus, they went to those theme parks and paid like fifteen bucks for a bottle of water, which is just absolute robbery if you ask me. I told him he should have just taken them camping up by the lake. We used to go fishing up there when we were kids, and you don't have to stand in line for two hours just to get on a ride. \n\nBut, uh... wait, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, being organized. Yeah, no, I don't really have an issue with that. I pretty much always know where my stuff is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah, restless is a good word for it. It's like... when I have to sit still, especially for a long time, it feels like this pressure just starts building up inside my chest and my legs. Like I've got to move or do something right that second or I'm gonna crawl out of my skin.

No, I don't think so. My hearing's fine, honestly. I don't really have the TV up super loud or anything. My wife is usually the one who wants it louder. She says she can't hear the dialogue over the background music in movies these days. Which, she has a point, they do mix the sound really weird lately. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI'm not really sure, to be honest. I mean, I definitely hear the sound of their voice. My hearing is actually perfectly fine. We have to do these mandatory hearing tests at the plant every year because of OSHA rules, right? They stick you in this tiny little soundproof booth. It always reminds me of those old payphone booths. You don't really see those anymore, do you? I saw one at a gas station out on Route 9 a few months ago and it didn't even have a phone in it, just wires hanging out. Everybody just uses cell phones now anyway. \n\nBut yeah, in the booth, you put on these huge headphones and you have to click a little button every time you hear a beep. I always pass with flying colors. Though last time the nurse said I had a little wax buildup. My buddy Dave—he drives the forklift on the loading dock—he told me I should try ear candling. Have you ever seen that? You light a hollow candle and stick it right in your ear canal. Sounds like a great way to set your hair on fire if you ask me. I told him he was crazy. \n\nBut wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the words. Yeah, I don't know, I guess I just don't process it or something. Like, I hear the noise they're making, but the meaning just kind of slips right by me.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

  "confirmed": [

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). He swore he was gonna fix it up himself, bought all this expensive welding gear and took over their whole two-car garage.
